    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’ve been... I don’t know, blowing up at people over things that shouldn’t set me off. Like on operations calls, I’ll snap at someone for a small mistake or a question, and as soon as it’s out of my mouth I can tell it was way too much. And then at home too, with my family, I’ll get sharp or angry over nothing, just little everyday stuff.\n\nThat’s not really me. I mean, I’ve had stressful jobs, I manage people, I’m used to pressure, but I’ve never handled things like this before. Lately it feels like my fuse is just gone, and I don’t like who I’m turning into.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah... anxious, but it’s not just like worrying in my head. It’s like my body is on standby all the time.\n\nAny time I hear certain sounds now — like a forklift backing up and beeping, or a compressor kicking on at work, even just the smoke alarm doing that little low-battery chirp at home — I tense up immediately. My shoulders go up, my chest gets tight, and before I even think about it I’m looking around for where the exits are, who’s nearby, what could go wrong next. It’s like I’m scanning the room without choosing to do it.\n\nAnd I know some of those sounds are normal. I’ve been around warehouses and equipment for years, so it’s not like I don’t know what a forklift sounds like. But lately it hits me like something bad is about to happen, and I can’t relax until I’ve checked everything. It’s exhausting, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about certain thoughts or images that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s actually one of the things that’s been scaring me the most.\n\nI can be doing something completely normal, like sitting at the kitchen table, or trying to answer emails from home, and out of nowhere it’s like I’m not really there anymore. I’m right back seeing that flash from the valve, and the smoke in the aisle. It just kind of shoves everything else out of my head.\n\nIt’s not like I’m choosing to think about it or replaying it on purpose. I’ll be trying to focus on a spreadsheet or read an email, and then suddenly that image is there, so clear that I lose track of what I was doing. I’ll just sit there staring for a second, and then I have to remind myself where I am. It’s... it feels like it barges in, I guess. I can’t just push it away when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what happened with the valve and the smoke in the aisle that you mentioned?\n\nPatient: I wish I could tell you straight through, but that’s part of what scares me. There are pieces of it I know because other people have told me, and then there are pieces that I can see really clearly, like the flash and the smoke in the aisle, but the actual order of what happened... it gets broken up.\n\nThe worst part is those seconds around the alarm and the shutdown. I know there was an alarm, I know people were moving, and I know I must’ve been doing something because the guys keep saying I did the right things, that I handled it, that I got people moving the way I was supposed to. But when I try to pull it up in my own head, it’s like there’s just a blank space. Not the whole thing, but chunks of it. Important chunks.\n\nAnd that bothers me because I’m the operations guy. I’m supposed to know what happened. I’m supposed to be able to walk through it step by step — valve, alarm, shutdown, who was where, what I said, what I did. But I can’t. I’ll get little flashes, then nothing, then maybe someone telling me later what I apparently did. And I keep thinking, how can I be told I did the right things and still not remember doing them? That doesn’t feel right. It makes me feel like I can’t trust my own head about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of what happened with the valve and the smoke in the aisle?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean… not in a way I’ve put together clearly.\n\nI haven’t sat down and said, “I’m avoiding this person” or “I’m not going there because of what happened.” It’s more like… I notice I hesitate around anything connected to that area or that conversation, but I don’t know if that counts as avoiding or if I’m just trying not to stir myself up.\n\nLike, when people start talking about the incident, I don’t exactly want to be part of that conversation. But I also don’t know that I’ve been deliberately dodging people. I’ve been kind of keeping my head down, I guess. And places… I’m not sure. I still know I have responsibilities, so it’s not like I can just say I’ll never be around equipment or alarms or that aisle again. But I can feel myself not wanting to get close to reminders of it.\n\nSo… maybe? I’m sorry, that’s not a clean answer. I haven’t really tracked it that way. I just know there are things I don’t want to deal with right now if they bring it all back.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty falling or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Very much.\n\nMost nights I’m exhausted, like my body feels completely done, but the second I lie down it’s like everything gets loud again. I’ll be staring at the ceiling, and I keep hearing that alarm in my head. Not even like a thought exactly — it’s like my brain keeps replaying the sound, and then I’m tense, waiting for something to happen, even though I’m in my own bedroom.\n\nAnd when I finally do drift off, it doesn’t last. I’ll wake up sweating, sometimes from dreams where there’s smoke everywhere and people are yelling, and I can’t quite get my bearings when I first wake up. I’ll sit up and it takes me a minute to realize I’m not there.\n\nBy morning it feels like I barely slept at all. Like I was in bed for hours, but I don’t feel rested. I feel wrung out before the day even starts.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these symptoms?\n\nPatient: It’s been about three weeks now. Pretty much since the valve failure happened, and since the injury at the facility.\n\nI keep trying to think if there was any gap where I felt normal afterward, but honestly it feels like it started right then and just… never really let up. The days kind of blur together, but it’s around three weeks. Ever since that incident.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly aware of your surroundings, even when there's no apparent danger?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I didn’t really think of it that way at first, but I’m constantly on alert now.\n\nLike, if I hear a forklift backing up, that beeping sound, my whole body just locks up. Same with a compressor kicking on — even if it’s just a normal sound and nothing is wrong, I immediately look around like something’s about to fail or start smoking again. I’ll start checking where the exits are, who’s near me, what equipment is running, what could go wrong next. It’s automatic before I’ve even had a chance to think.\n\nAnd it’s not just at work. At home, if the smoke alarm chirps or makes any little sound, I tense up right away. I know, logically, a low battery chirp isn’t the same thing, but my body doesn’t seem to know that. I’m already scanning the room, listening for more, waiting for some other sign that there’s danger.\n\nIt feels like I can’t just be in a place anymore. I’m always watching and listening for the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for what happened with the valve failure?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nPart of me wants to say no, because I know I didn’t make the valve fail with my own hands or anything like that. I keep telling myself that. But then my head goes back over every little thing, like… should I have noticed something earlier, should I have pushed harder, should I have stopped the line sooner, should I have been standing in a different place, should I have told somebody something differently.\n\nSo, responsible? I don’t know. I don’t know if that’s the right word. But I do keep going back through it like there’s some version where if I’d done one small thing differently, maybe it wouldn’t have gone the way it did. And I know that might not be rational, but it’s there. It’s hard not to feel like, as the person in charge of operations, I should’ve somehow prevented it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly aware of your surroundings, even when there's no apparent danger, in quiet or safe environments like your home?\n\nPatient: Yes. Especially at home, which is what bothers me, because home is supposed to be the place where I can shut all that off.\n\nIf the smoke alarm chirps, even just that little low-battery kind of chirp, my whole body tightens up before I even think. I’ll stop what I’m doing and start listening for more sounds, looking around the room, checking where the exits are, thinking, okay, what’s running, what could be overheating, what could fail. And I know it sounds excessive, because nothing is usually happening. It’s quiet, it’s safe, there’s no smoke, no emergency. But my body reacts like something is about to go wrong.\n\nIt’s the same kind of thing as when I hear a forklift beep or a compressor kick on at work — that sound hits me and I’m already scanning, already bracing. I don’t feel like I’m choosing to do it. I feel wrung out before the day even starts.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these symptoms?\n\nPatient: It’s been about three weeks now. Pretty much since the valve failure happened, and since the injury at the facility.\n\nI keep trying to think if there was any gap where I felt normal afterward, but honestly it feels like it started right then and just… never really let up. The days kind of blur together, but it’s around three weeks. Ever since that incident.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly aware of your surroundings, even when there's no apparent danger?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I didn’t really think of it that way at first, but I’m constantly on alert now.\n\nLike, if I hear a forklift backing up, that beeping sound, my whole body just locks up. Same with a compressor kicking on — even if it’s just a normal sound and nothing is wrong, I immediately look around like something’s about to fail or start smoking again. I’ll start checking where the exits are, who’s near me, what equipment is running, what could go wrong next. It’s automatic before I’ve even had a chance to think.\n\nAnd it’s not just at work. At home, if the smoke alarm chirps or makes any little sound, I tense up right away. I know, logically, a low battery chirp isn’t the same thing, but my body doesn’t seem to know that. I’m already scanning the room, listening for more, waiting for some other sign that there’s danger.\n\nIt feels like I can’t just be in a place anymore. I’m always watching and listening for the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for what happened with the valve failure?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nPart of me wants to say no, because I know I didn’t make the valve fail with my own hands or anything like that. I keep telling myself that. But then my head goes back over every little thing, like… should I have noticed something earlier, should I have pushed harder, should I have stopped the line sooner, should I have been standing in a different place, should I have told somebody something differently.\n\nSo, responsible? I don’t know. I don’t know if that’s the right word. But I do keep going back through it like there’s some version where if I’d done one small thing differently, maybe it wouldn’t have gone the way it did. And I know that might not be rational, but it’s there. It’s hard not to feel like, as the person in charge of operations, I should’ve somehow prevented it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly aware of your surroundings, even when there's no apparent danger, in quiet or safe environments like your home?\n\nPatient: Yes. Especially at home, which is what bothers me, because home is supposed to be the place where I can shut all that off.\n\nIf the smoke alarm chirps, even just that little low-battery kind of chirp, my whole body tightens up before I even think. I’ll stop what I’m doing and start listening for more sounds, looking around the room, checking where the exits are, thinking, okay, what’s running, what could be overheating, what could fail. And I know it sounds excessive, because nothing is usually happening. It’s quiet, it’s safe, there’s no smoke, no emergency. But my body reacts like something is about to go wrong.\n\nIt’s the same kind of thing as when I hear a forklift beep or a compressor kick on at work — that sound hits me and I’m already scanning, already bracing. I don’t feel like I’m choosing to do it. It’s just automatic now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, or like things aren't real?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’d describe it exactly like being outside my body, like watching myself from across the room or anything.\n\nThere are moments where I feel kind of… not fully there, maybe? But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I’m so caught up in reacting or trying to figure out what’s happening that afterward it feels a little blurry, like I was on autopilot. But I can’t say I’ve had this clear feeling of floating above myself or looking down at myself.\n\nAnd as far as things not being real… I don’t know. I don’t think so. I mean, I know where I am. I know what’s going on. It’s more like I get overwhelmed or locked into what I’m noticing, not like the room turns unreal or anything. Maybe I’m misunderstanding the question, but I haven’t really noticed it in that specific way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when you're reliving the memory of the valve failure and the smoke in the aisle?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that I feel detached, exactly. When the memory comes back, it’s not like I’m looking at myself from the outside or like the room disappears or becomes unreal. I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way.\n\nIt’s more that I get pulled into the details of it, if that makes sense. Like my mind goes straight to the aisle, the smoke, the valve, what I was trying to figure out, what I should’ve done next. But I still know I’m here. I know where I am. I don’t feel separated from myself so much as… overwhelmed by it, or stuck on it for a bit.\n\nI guess I hesitate because maybe there are moments where I go quiet or feel kind of far away, but I don’t know if that’s detachment or just me being caught in the memory. I wouldn’t want to say yes if you mean something more specific than that.\n\nDoctor: When you're remembering what happened with the valve failure and the smoke in the aisle, do you ever feel like you're not really there or like it's not happening to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that exact way. When I remember it, it feels like it’s definitely happening to me, if that makes sense. I don’t feel like I’m watching some other person, or like I’m not really part of it.\n\nIt’s more like my head gets crowded with the scene — the aisle, the smoke, trying to understand what failed, what I was supposed to do — but I still know it’s my memory. I know I’m sitting here or standing in my kitchen or wherever I am at the time. I don’t lose track of that completely.\n\nMaybe I get quiet, or I zone in too hard on it, and afterward I might think, okay, I was kind of gone for a minute. It’s more like I’m too much there with the memory, not separate from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been deliberately avoiding any specific activities or places that remind you of the valve failure incident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve made a clear decision like, “I’m never going there again” or “I’m not doing that activity,” if that’s what you mean by deliberately avoiding.\n\nThere are definitely things that make me think of it, but I don’t know that I can point to a specific place I’ve been avoiding on purpose. Sometimes I hesitate or kind of drag my feet if something feels like it’s going to put me back in that mindset, but I don’t know if that counts. It’s not like I have a formal list in my head.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t noticed a specific activity or place that I’m consciously steering around because of the valve failure. If I am doing it, it may be more automatic than deliberate, but I can’t say for sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes. I mean… yes, that’s probably the right word for it. It doesn’t feel like just thinking about it on purpose. It can happen when I’m doing something completely normal, like sitting at the kitchen table, or trying to answer emails from home, and then suddenly it’s like I’m right back there.

I’ll see that flash from the valve, and the smoke in the aisle, and it just kind of shoves everything else out of my head. Whatever I was doing a second before almost doesn’t matter for a moment. It’s not me choosing to replay it. It just comes in hard, and it’s very clear, like my mind snaps back to that scene before I can stop it.

Sometimes I’ll realize I’ve stopped typing or I’m just sitting there staring because I’m caught in that image again. It’s not like a normal memory where you can keep it at a distance. It feels immediate, like it takes over my attention.

Very much.\n\nMost nights I’m exhausted, like my body feels completely done, but the second I lie down it’s like everything gets loud again. I’ll be staring at the ceiling, and I keep hearing that alarm in my head. Not even like a thought exactly — it’s like my brain keeps replaying the sound, and then I’m tense, waiting for something to happen, even though I’m in my own bedroom.\n\nAnd when I finally do drift off, it doesn’t last. I feel wrung out before the day even starts.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these symptoms?\n\nPatient: It’s been about three weeks now. Pretty much since the valve failure happened, and since the injury at the facility.\n\nI keep trying to think if there was any gap where I felt normal afterward, but honestly it feels like it started right then and just… never really let up. The days kind of blur together, but it’s around three weeks. Ever since that incident.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly aware of your surroundings, even when there's no apparent danger?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I didn’t really think of it that way at first, but I’m constantly on alert now.\n\nLike, if I hear a forklift backing up, that beeping sound, my whole body just locks up. Same with a compressor kicking on — even if it’s just a normal sound and nothing is wrong, I immediately look around like something’s about to fail or start smoking again. I’ll start checking where the exits are, who’s near me, what equipment is running, what could go wrong next. It’s automatic before I’ve even had a chance to think.\n\nAnd it’s not just at work. At home, if the smoke alarm chirps or makes any little sound, I tense up right away. I know, logically, a low battery chirp isn’t the same thing, but my body doesn’t seem to know that. I’m already scanning the room, listening for more, waiting for some other sign that there’s danger.\n\nIt feels like I can’t just be in a place anymore. I’m always watching and listening for the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for what happened with the valve failure?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nPart of me wants to say no, because I know I didn’t make the valve fail with my own hands or anything like that. I keep telling myself that. But then my head goes back over every little thing, like… should I have noticed something earlier, should I have pushed harder, should I have stopped the line sooner, should I have been standing in a different place, should I have told somebody something differently.\n\nSo, responsible? I don’t know. I don’t know if that’s the right word. But I do keep going back through it like there’s some version where if I’d done one small thing differently, maybe it wouldn’t have gone the way it did. And I know that might not be rational, but it’s there. It’s hard not to feel like, as the person in charge of operations, I should’ve somehow prevented it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly aware of your surroundings, even when there's no apparent danger, in quiet or safe environments like your home?\n\nPatient: Yes. Especially at home, which is what bothers me, because home is supposed to be the place where I can shut all that off.\n\nIf the smoke alarm chirps, even just that little low-battery kind of chirp, my whole body tightens up before I even think. I’ll stop what I’m doing and start listening for more sounds, looking around the room, checking where the exits are, thinking, okay, what’s running, what could be overheating, what could fail. And I know it sounds excessive, because nothing is usually happening. It’s quiet, it’s safe, there’s no smoke, no emergency. But my body reacts like something is about to go wrong.\n\nIt’s the same kind of thing as when I hear a forklift beep or a compressor kick on at work — that sound hits me and I’m already scanning, already bracing. I don’t feel like I’m choosing to do it. It’s just automatic now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, or like things aren't real?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’d describe it exactly like being outside my body, like watching myself from across the room or anything.\n\nThere are moments where I feel kind of… not fully there, maybe? But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I’m so caught up in reacting or trying to figure out what’s happening that afterward it feels a little blurry, like I was on autopilot. But I can’t say I’ve had this clear feeling of floating above myself or looking down at myself.\n\nAnd as far as things not being real… I don’t know. I don’t think so. I mean, I know where I am. I know what’s going on. It’s more like I get overwhelmed or locked into what I’m noticing, not like the room turns unreal or anything. Maybe I’m misunderstanding the question, but I haven’t really noticed it in that specific way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when you're reliving the memory of the valve failure and the smoke in the aisle?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that I feel detached, exactly. When the memory comes back, it’s not like I’m looking at myself from the outside or like the room disappears or becomes unreal. I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way.\n\nIt’s more that I get pulled into the details of it, if that makes sense. Like my mind goes straight to the aisle, the smoke, the valve, what I was trying to figure out, what I should’ve done next. But I still know I’m here. I know where I am. I don’t feel separated from myself so much as… overwhelmed by it, or stuck on it for a bit.\n\nI guess I hesitate because maybe there are moments where I go quiet or feel kind of far away, but I don’t know if that’s detachment or just me being caught in the memory. I wouldn’t want to say yes if you mean something more specific than that.\n\nDoctor: When you're remembering what happened with the valve failure and the smoke in the aisle, do you ever feel like you're not really there or like it's not happening to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that exact way. When I remember it, it feels like it’s definitely happening to me, if that makes sense. I don’t feel like I’m watching some other person, or like I’m not really part of it.\n\nIt’s more like my head gets crowded with the scene — the aisle, the smoke, trying to understand what failed, what I was supposed to do — but I still know it’s my memory. I know I’m sitting here or standing in my kitchen or wherever I am at the time. I don’t lose track of that completely.\n\nMaybe I get quiet, or I zone in too hard on it, and afterward I might think, okay, I was kind of gone for a minute. But I don’t know that I’d call it “not really there.” I wouldn’t want to say yes to that and make it sound like I’m having some out-of-body thing, because I haven’t noticed that. It’s more like I’m too much there with the memory, not separate from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been deliberately avoiding any specific activities or places that remind you of the valve failure incident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve made a clear decision like, “I’m never going there again” or “I’m not doing that activity,” if that’s what you mean by deliberately avoiding.\n\nThere are definitely things that make me think of it, but I don’t know that I can point to a specific place I’ve been avoiding on purpose. Sometimes I hesitate or kind of drag my feet if something feels like it’s going to put me back in that mindset, but I don’t know if that counts. It’s not like I have a formal list in my head.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t noticed a specific activity or place that I’m consciously steering around because of the valve failure. If I am doing it, it may be more automatic than deliberate, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience flashbacks to the valve failure incident?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, that’s probably the right word for it. It doesn’t feel like just thinking about it on purpose. It can happen when I’m doing something completely normal, like sitting at the kitchen table, or trying to answer emails from home, and then suddenly it’s like I’m right back there.\n\nI’ll see that flash from the valve, and the smoke in the aisle, and it just kind of shoves everything else out of my head. Whatever I was doing a second before almost doesn’t matter for a moment. It’s not me choosing to replay it. It just comes in hard, and it’s very clear, like my mind snaps back to that scene before I can stop it.\n\nSometimes I’ll realize I’ve stopped typing or I’m just sitting there staring because I’m caught in that image again. It’s not like a normal memory where you can keep it at a distance. It feels immediate, like it takes over my attention.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It feels immediate, like it takes over my attention.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel guilty about what happened during the valve failure incident?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. When I remember it, it feels like it’s definitely happening to me, if that makes sense. I don’t feel like I’m watching some other person, or like I’m not really part of it.\n\nIt’s more like my head gets crowded with the scene — the aisle, the smoke, trying to understand what failed, what I was supposed to do — but I still know it’s my memory. I know I’m sitting here or standing in my kitchen or wherever I am at the time. I don’t lose track of that completely.\n\nMaybe I get quiet, or I zone in too hard on it, and afterward I might think, okay, I was kind of gone for a minute. But I don’t know that I’d call it “not really there.” I wouldn’t want to say yes to that and make it sound like I’m having some out-of-body thing, because I haven’t noticed that. It’s more like I’m too much there with the memory, not separate from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been deliberately avoiding any specific activities or places that remind you of the valve failure incident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve made a clear decision like, “I’m never going there again” or “I’m not doing that activity,” if that’s what you mean by deliberately avoiding.\n\nThere are definitely things that make me think of it, but I don’t know that I can point to a specific place I’ve been avoiding on purpose. Sometimes I hesitate or kind of drag my feet if something feels like it’s going to put me back in that mindset, but I don’t know if that counts. It’s not like I have a formal list in my head.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t noticed a specific activity or place that I’m consciously steering around because of the valve failure. I mean… yes, that’s probably the right word for it. It doesn’t feel like just thinking about it on purpose. It can happen when I’m doing something completely normal, like sitting at the kitchen table, or trying to answer emails from home, and then suddenly it’s like I’m right back there.\n\nI’ll see that flash from the valve, and the smoke in the aisle, and it just kind of shoves everything else out of my head. Whatever I was doing a second before almost doesn’t matter for a moment. It’s not me choosing to replay it. It just comes in hard, and it’s very clear, like my mind snaps back to that scene before I can stop it.\n\nSometimes I’ll realize I’ve stopped typing or I’m just sitting there staring because I’m caught in that image again. It feels immediate, like it takes over my attention.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel guilty about what happened during the valve failure incident?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 40 / general_or_operations_manager / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. “Guilty” is a strong word, and I’m not sure if what I feel is guilt or just… going over it again and again and wondering if I should have done something differently.

I mean, I keep thinking about the sequence of it, like where I was, what I noticed first, what I understood in that moment versus what I understand now. And I’ll catch myself thinking, “Did I miss something? Was there some sign earlier? Should I have reacted faster?” But I don’t know if that means I feel guilty exactly, or if I’m just trying to make sense of it.

I haven’t sat there and said, “This was my fault,” not in a straightforward way. But it’s hard not to have those thoughts creep in, especially because I’m used to being responsible for operations and keeping things under control. So when something like that happens, even if I don’t know that I caused it, my mind still goes to what I could’ve controlled.

So… maybe some guilt-adjacent feelings, if that makes sense. But I’m not sure I’d say plain guilt. It’s more questioning myself than a clear feeling that I did something wrong.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’ve been... I don’t know, blowing up at people over things that shouldn’t set me off. Like on operations calls, I’ll snap at someone for a small mistake or a question, and as soon as it’s out of my mouth I can tell it was way too much. And then at home too, with my family, I’ll get sharp or angry over nothing, just little everyday stuff.\n\nThat’s not really me. I mean, I’ve had stressful jobs, I manage people, I’m used to pressure, but I’ve never handled things like this before. Lately it feels like my fuse is just gone, and I don’t like who I’m turning into.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah... anxious, but it’s not just like worrying in my head. It’s like my body is on standby all the time.\n\nAny time I hear certain sounds now — like a forklift backing up and beeping, or a compressor kicking on at work, even just the smoke alarm doing that little low-battery chirp at home — I tense up immediately. My shoulders go up, my chest gets tight, and before I even think about it I’m looking around for where the exits are, who’s nearby, what could go wrong next. It’s like I’m scanning the room without choosing to do it.\n\nAnd I know some of those sounds are normal. I’ve been around warehouses and equipment for years, so it’s not like I don’t know what a forklift sounds like. But lately it hits me like something bad is about to happen, and I can’t relax until I’ve checked everything. It’s exhausting, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about certain thoughts or images that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s actually one of the things that’s been scaring me the most.\n\nI can be doing something completely normal, like sitting at the kitchen table, or trying to answer emails from home, and out of nowhere it’s like I’m not really there anymore. I’m right back seeing that flash from the valve, and the smoke in the aisle. It just kind of shoves everything else out of my head.\n\nIt’s not like I’m choosing to think about it or replaying it on purpose. I’ll be trying to focus on a spreadsheet or read an email, and then suddenly that image is there, so clear that I lose track of what I was doing. I’ll just sit there staring for a second, and then I have to remind myself where I am. It’s... it feels like it barges in, I guess. I can’t just push it away when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what happened with the valve and the smoke in the aisle that you mentioned?\n\nPatient: I wish I could tell you straight through, but that’s part of what scares me. There are pieces of it I know because other people have told me, and then there are pieces that I can see really clearly, like the flash and the smoke in the aisle, but the actual order of what happened... it gets broken up.\n\nThe worst part is those seconds around the alarm and the shutdown. I know there was an alarm, I know people were moving, and I know I must’ve been doing something because the guys keep saying I did the right things, that I handled it, that I got people moving the way I was supposed to. But when I try to pull it up in my own head, it’s like there’s just a blank space. Not the whole thing, but chunks of it. Important chunks.\n\nAnd that bothers me because I’m the operations guy. I’m supposed to know what happened. I’m supposed to be able to walk through it step by step — valve, alarm, shutdown, who was where, what I said, what I did. But I can’t. I’ll get little flashes, then nothing, then maybe someone telling me later what I apparently did. And I keep thinking, how can I be told I did the right things and still not remember doing them? That doesn’t feel right. It makes me feel like I can’t trust my own head about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of what happened with the valve and the smoke in the aisle?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean… not in a way I’ve put together clearly.\n\nI haven’t sat down and said, “I’m avoiding this person” or “I’m not going there because of what happened.” It’s more like… I notice I hesitate around anything connected to that area or that conversation, but I don’t know if that counts as avoiding or if I’m just trying not to stir myself up.\n\nLike, when people start talking about the incident, I don’t exactly want to be part of that conversation. But I also don’t know that I’ve been deliberately dodging people. I’ve been kind of keeping my head down, I guess. And places… I’m not sure. I still know I have responsibilities, so it’s not like I can just say I’ll never be around equipment or alarms or that aisle again. But I can feel myself not wanting to get close to reminders of it.\n\nSo… maybe? I’m sorry, that’s not a clean answer. I haven’t really tracked it that way. I just know there are things I don’t want to deal with right now if they bring it all back.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty falling or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Very much.\n\nMost nights I’m exhausted, like my body feels completely done, but the second I lie down it’s like everything gets loud again. I’ll be staring at the ceiling, and I keep hearing that alarm in my head. Not even like a thought exactly — it’s like my brain keeps replaying the sound, and then I’m tense, waiting for something to happen, even though I’m in my own bedroom.\n\nAnd when I finally do drift off, it doesn’t last. I’ll wake up sweating, sometimes from dreams where there’s smoke everywhere and people are yelling, and I can’t quite get my bearings when I first wake up. I’ll sit up and it takes me a minute to realize I’m not there.\n\nBy morning it feels like I barely slept at all. Like I was in bed for hours, but I don’t feel rested. I feel wrung out before the day even starts.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these symptoms?\n\nPatient: It’s been about three weeks now. Pretty much since the valve failure happened, and since the injury at the facility.\n\nI keep trying to think if there was any gap where I felt normal afterward, but honestly it feels like it started right then and just… never really let up. The days kind of blur together, but it’s around three weeks. Ever since that incident.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly aware of your surroundings, even when there's no apparent danger?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I didn’t really think of it that way at first, but I’m constantly on alert now.\n\nLike, if I hear a forklift backing up, that beeping sound, my whole body just locks up. Same with a compressor kicking on — even if it’s just a normal sound and nothing is wrong, I immediately look around like something’s about to fail or start smoking again. I’ll start checking where the exits are, who’s near me, what equipment is running, what could go wrong next. It’s automatic before I’ve even had a chance to think.\n\nAnd it’s not just at work. At home, if the smoke alarm chirps or makes any little sound, I tense up right away. I know, logically, a low battery chirp isn’t the same thing, but my body doesn’t seem to know that. I’m already scanning the room, listening for more, waiting for some other sign that there’s danger.\n\nIt feels like I can’t just be in a place anymore. I’m always watching and listening for the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for what happened with the valve failure?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nPart of me wants to say no, because I know I didn’t make the valve fail with my own hands or anything like that. I keep telling myself that. But then my head goes back over every little thing, like… should I have noticed something earlier, should I have pushed harder, should I have stopped the line sooner, should I have been standing in a different place, should I have told somebody something differently.\n\nSo, responsible? I don’t know. I don’t know if that’s the right word. But I do keep going back through it like there’s some version where if I’d done one small thing differently, maybe it wouldn’t have gone the way it did. And I know that might not be rational, but it’s there. It’s hard not to feel like, as the person in charge of operations, I should’ve somehow prevented it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly aware of your surroundings, even when there's no apparent danger, in quiet or safe environments like your home?\n\nPatient: Yes. Especially at home, which is what bothers me, because home is supposed to be the place where I can shut all that off.\n\nIf the smoke alarm chirps, even just that little low-battery kind of chirp, my whole body tightens up before I even think. I’ll stop what I’m doing and start listening for more sounds, looking around the room, checking where the exits are, thinking, okay, what’s running, what could be overheating, what could fail. And I know it sounds excessive, because nothing is usually happening. It’s quiet, it’s safe, there’s no smoke, no emergency. But my body reacts like something is about to go wrong.\n\nIt’s the same kind of thing as when I hear a forklift beep or a compressor kick on at work — that sound hits me and I’m already scanning, already bracing. I don’t feel like I’m choosing to do it. It’s just automatic now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, or like things aren't real?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’d describe it exactly like being outside my body, like watching myself from across the room or anything.\n\nThere are moments where I feel kind of… not fully there, maybe? But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I’m so caught up in reacting or trying to figure out what’s happening that afterward it feels a little blurry, like I was on autopilot. But I can’t say I’ve had this clear feeling of floating above myself or looking down at myself.\n\nAnd as far as things not being real… I don’t know. I don’t think so. I mean, I know where I am. I know what’s going on. It’s more like I get overwhelmed or locked into what I’m noticing, not like the room turns unreal or anything. Maybe I’m misunderstanding the question, but I haven’t really noticed it in that specific way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when you're reliving the memory of the valve failure and the smoke in the aisle?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that I feel detached, exactly. When the memory comes back, it’s not like I’m looking at myself from the outside or like the room disappears or becomes unreal. I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way.\n\nIt’s more that I get pulled into the details of it, if that makes sense. Like my mind goes straight to the aisle, the smoke, the valve, what I was trying to figure out, what I should’ve done next. But I still know I’m here. I know where I am. I don’t feel separated from myself so much as… overwhelmed by it, or stuck on it for a bit.\n\nI guess I hesitate because maybe there are moments where I go quiet or feel kind of far away, but I don’t know if that’s detachment or just me being caught in the memory. I wouldn’t want to say yes if you mean something more specific than that.\n\nDoctor: When you're remembering what happened with the valve failure and the smoke in the aisle, do you ever feel like you're not really there or like it's not happening to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that exact way. When I remember it, it feels like it’s definitely happening to me, if that makes sense. I don’t feel like I’m watching some other person, or like I’m not really part of it.\n\nIt’s more like my head gets crowded with the scene — the aisle, the smoke, trying to understand what failed, what I was supposed to do — but I still know it’s my memory. I know I’m sitting here or standing in my kitchen or wherever I am at the time. I don’t lose track of that completely.\n\nMaybe I get quiet, or I zone in too hard on it, and afterward I might think, okay, I was kind of gone for a minute. But I don’t know that I’d call it “not really there.” I wouldn’t want to say yes to that and make it sound like I’m having some out-of-body thing, because I haven’t noticed that. It’s more like I’m too much there with the memory, not separate from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been deliberately avoiding any specific activities or places that remind you of the valve failure incident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve made a clear decision like, “I’m never going there again” or “I’m not doing that activity,” if that’s what you mean by deliberately avoiding.\n\nThere are definitely things that make me think of it, but I don’t know that I can point to a specific place I’ve been avoiding on purpose. But I also don’t know that I’ve been deliberately dodging people. I’ve been kind of keeping my head down, I guess. And places… I’m not sure. I still know I have responsibilities, so it’s not like I can just say I’ll never be around equipment or alarms or that aisle again. But I can feel myself not wanting to get close to reminders of it.\n\nSo… maybe? I’m sorry, that’s not a clean answer. I haven’t really tracked it that way. I just know there are things I don’t want to deal with right now if they bring it all back.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty falling or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Very much.\n\nMost nights I’m exhausted, like my body feels completely done, but the second I lie down it’s like everything gets loud again. I’ll be staring at the ceiling, and I keep hearing that alarm in my head. Not even like a thought exactly — it’s like my brain keeps replaying the sound, and then I’m tense, waiting for something to happen, even though I’m in my own bedroom.\n\nAnd when I finally do drift off, it doesn’t last. I’ll wake up sweating, sometimes from dreams where there’s smoke everywhere and people are yelling, and I can’t quite get my bearings when I first wake up. I’ll sit up and it takes me a minute to realize I’m not there.\n\nBy morning it feels like I barely slept at all. Like I was in bed for hours, but I don’t feel rested. I feel wrung out before the day even starts.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these symptoms?\n\nPatient: It’s been about three weeks now. Pretty much since the valve failure happened, and since the injury at the facility.\n\nI keep trying to think if there was any gap where I felt normal afterward, but honestly it feels like it started right then and just… never really let up. The days kind of blur together, but it’s around three weeks. Ever since that incident.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly aware of your surroundings, even when there's no apparent danger?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I didn’t really think of it that way at first, but I’m constantly on alert now.\n\nLike, if I hear a forklift backing up, that beeping sound, my whole body just locks up. Same with a compressor kicking on — even if it’s just a normal sound and nothing is wrong, I immediately look around like something’s about to fail or start smoking again. I’ll start checking where the exits are, who’s near me, what equipment is running, what could go wrong next. It’s automatic before I’ve even had a chance to think.\n\nAnd it’s not just at work. At home, if the smoke alarm chirps or makes any little sound, I tense up right away. I know, logically, a low battery chirp isn’t the same thing, but my body doesn’t seem to know that. I’m already scanning the room, listening for more, waiting for some other sign that there’s danger.\n\nIt feels like I can’t just be in a place anymore. I’m always watching and listening for the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for what happened with the valve failure?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nPart of me wants to say no, because I know I didn’t make the valve fail with my own hands or anything like that. I keep telling myself that. But then my head goes back over every little thing, like… should I have noticed something earlier, should I have pushed harder, should I have stopped the line sooner, should I have been standing in a different place, should I have told somebody something differently.\n\nSo, responsible? I don’t know. I don’t know if that’s the right word. But I do keep going back through it like there’s some version where if I’d done one small thing differently, maybe it wouldn’t have gone the way it did. And I know that might not be rational, but it’s there. It’s hard not to feel like, as the person in charge of operations, I should’ve somehow prevented it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly aware of your surroundings, even when there's no apparent danger, in quiet or safe environments like your home?\n\nPatient: Yes. Especially at home, which is what bothers me, because home is supposed to be the place where I can shut all that off.\n\nIf the smoke alarm chirps, even just that little low-battery kind of chirp, my whole body tightens up before I even think. I’ll stop what I’m doing and start listening for more sounds, looking around the room, checking where the exits are, thinking, okay, what’s running, what could be overheating, what could fail. And I know it sounds excessive, because nothing is usually happening. It’s quiet, it’s safe, there’s no smoke, no emergency. But my body reacts like something is about to go wrong.\n\nIt’s the same kind of thing as when I hear a forklift beep or a compressor kick on at work — that sound hits me and I’m already scanning, already bracing. I don’t feel like I’m choosing to do it. It’s just automatic now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, or like things aren't real?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’d describe it exactly like being outside my body, like watching myself from across the room or anything.\n\nThere are moments where I feel kind of… not fully there, maybe? But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I’m so caught up in reacting or trying to figure out what’s happening that afterward it feels a little blurry, like I was on autopilot. But I can’t say I’ve had this clear feeling of floating above myself or looking down at myself.\n\nAnd as far as things not being real… I don’t know. I don’t think so. I mean, I know where I am. I know what’s going on. It’s more like I get overwhelmed or locked into what I’m noticing, not like the room turns unreal or anything. Maybe I’m misunderstanding the question, but I haven’t really noticed it in that specific way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when you're reliving the memory of the valve failure and the smoke in the aisle?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that I feel detached, exactly. When the memory comes back, it’s not like I’m looking at myself from the outside or like the room disappears or becomes unreal. I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way.\n\nIt’s more that I get pulled into the details of it, if that makes sense. Like my mind goes straight to the aisle, the smoke, the valve, what I was trying to figure out, what I should’ve done next. But I still know I’m here. I know where I am. I don’t feel separated from myself so much as… overwhelmed by it, or stuck on it for a bit.\n\nI guess I hesitate because maybe there are moments where I go quiet or feel kind of far away, but I don’t know if that’s detachment or just me being caught in the memory. I wouldn’t want to say yes if you mean something more specific than that.\n\nDoctor: When you're remembering what happened with the valve failure and the smoke in the aisle, do you ever feel like you're not really there or like it's not happening to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that exact way. When I remember it, it feels like it’s definitely happening to me, if that makes sense. I don’t feel like I’m watching some other person, or like I’m not really part of it.\n\nIt’s more like my head gets crowded with the scene — the aisle, the smoke, trying to understand what failed, what I was supposed to do — but I still know it’s my memory. I know I’m sitting here or standing in my kitchen or wherever I am at the time. I don’t lose track of that completely.\n\nMaybe I get quiet, or I zone in too hard on it, and afterward I might think, okay, I was kind of gone for a minute. But I don’t know that I’d call it “not really there.” I wouldn’t want to say yes to that and make it sound like I’m having some out-of-body thing, because I haven’t noticed that. It’s more like I’m too much there with the memory, not separate from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been deliberately avoiding any specific activities or places that remind you of the valve failure incident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve made a clear decision like, “I’m never going there again” or “I’m not doing that activity,” if that’s what you mean by deliberately avoiding.\n\nThere are definitely things that make me think of it, but I don’t know that I can point to a specific place I’ve been avoiding on purpose. Sometimes I hesitate or kind of drag my feet if something feels like it’s going to put me back in that mindset, but I don’t know if that counts. It’s not like I have a formal list in my head.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t noticed a specific activity or place that I’m consciously steering around because of the valve failure. If I am doing it, it may be more automatic than deliberate, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience flashbacks to the valve failure incident?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, that’s probably the right word for it. It doesn’t feel like just thinking about it on purpose. Whatever I was doing a second before almost doesn’t matter for a moment. It’s not me choosing to replay it. It just comes in hard, and it’s very clear, like my mind snaps back to that scene before I can stop it.\n\nSometimes I’ll realize I’ve stopped typing or I’m just sitting there staring because I’m caught in that image again. It’s not like a normal memory where you can keep it at a distance. It feels immediate, like it takes over my attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the valve failure incident?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. “Guilty” is a strong word, and I’m not sure if what I feel is guilt or just… going over it again and again and wondering if I should have done something differently.\n\nI mean, I keep thinking about the sequence of it, like where I was, what I noticed first, what I understood in that moment versus what I understand now. Yes. Yeah, I do. More than I used to, definitely.

It’s like my body reacts before I’ve even had time to think. Any time I hear a forklift backing up, that beeping sound, I tense up immediately. Or if a compressor kicks on and makes that sudden mechanical noise, I’m already scanning around, like, where is it coming from, is something failing, what’s the nearest way out, who’s standing too close to what. It’s not just at work either. Even at home, if the smoke alarm chirps because the battery is low, I feel my chest and shoulders tighten, and I’m looking around like something’s about to happen.

I know, logically, that a smoke alarm chirp at home isn’t the same thing. Or a normal forklift beep doesn’t mean there’s danger right then. But in the moment it doesn’t feel normal. It feels like I have to catch the next thing before it catches me. I’ll start looking for exits or thinking through what could go wrong next, almost automatically. It’s exhausting, honestly, because most people probably just hear a noise and move on, and I’m already bracing like something bad is starting again.

I’ll be trying to focus on a spreadsheet or read an email, and then suddenly that image is there, so clear that I lose track of what I was doing. I’ll just sit there staring for a second, and then I have to remind myself where I am. It’s... it feels like it barges in, I guess. I can’t just push it away when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what happened with the valve and the smoke in the aisle that you mentioned?\n\nPatient: I wish I could tell you straight through, but that’s part of what scares me. There are pieces of it I know because other people have told me, and then there are pieces that I can see really clearly, like the flash and the smoke in the aisle, but the actual order of what happened... it gets broken up.\n\nThe worst part is those seconds around the alarm and the shutdown. I know there was an alarm, I know people were moving, and I know I must’ve been doing something because the guys keep saying I did the right things, that I handled it, that I got people moving the way I was supposed to. But when I try to pull it up in my own head, it’s like there’s just a blank space. Not the whole thing, but chunks of it. Important chunks.\n\nAnd that bothers me because I’m the operations guy. I’m supposed to know what happened. I’m supposed to be able to walk through it step by step — valve, alarm, shutdown, who was where, what I said, what I did. But I can’t. I’ll get little flashes, then nothing, then maybe someone telling me later what I apparently did. And I keep thinking, how can I be told I did the right things and still not remember doing them? That doesn’t feel right. It makes me feel like I can’t trust my own head about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of what happened with the valve and the smoke in the aisle?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean… not in a way I’ve put together clearly.\n\nI haven’t sat down and said, “I’m avoiding this person” or “I’m not going there because of what happened.” It’s more like… I notice I hesitate around anything connected to that area or that conversation, but I don’t know if that counts as avoiding or if I’m just trying not to stir myself up.\n\nLike, when people start talking about the incident, I don’t exactly want to be part of that conversation. But I also don’t know that I’ve been deliberately dodging people. I’ve been kind of keeping my head down, I guess. And places… I’m not sure. I still know I have responsibilities, so it’s not like I can just say I’ll never be around equipment or alarms or that aisle again. But I can feel myself not wanting to get close to reminders of it.\n\nSo… maybe? I’m sorry, that’s not a clean answer. I haven’t really tracked it that way. I just know there are things I don’t want to deal with right now if they bring it all back.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty falling or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Very much.\n\nMost nights I’m exhausted, like my body feels completely done, but the second I lie down it’s like everything gets loud again. I’ll be staring at the ceiling, and I keep hearing that alarm in my head. Not even like a thought exactly — it’s like my brain keeps replaying the sound, and then I’m tense, waiting for something to happen, even though I’m in my own bedroom.\n\nAnd when I finally do drift off, it doesn’t last. I’ll wake up sweating, sometimes from dreams where there’s smoke everywhere and people are yelling, and I can’t quite get my bearings when I first wake up. I’ll sit up and it takes me a minute to realize I’m not there.\n\nBy morning it feels like I barely slept at all. Like I was in bed for hours, but I don’t feel rested. I feel wrung out before the day even starts.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these symptoms?\n\nPatient: It’s been about three weeks now. Pretty much since the valve failure happened, and since the injury at the facility.\n\nI keep trying to think if there was any gap where I felt normal afterward, but honestly it feels like it started right then and just… never really let up. The days kind of blur together, but it’s around three weeks. Ever since that incident.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly aware of your surroundings, even when there's no apparent danger?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I didn’t really think of it that way at first, but I’m constantly on alert now.\n\nLike, if I hear a forklift backing up, that beeping sound, my whole body just locks up. Same with a compressor kicking on — even if it’s just a normal sound and nothing is wrong, I immediately look around like something’s about to fail or start smoking again. I’ll start checking where the exits are, who’s near me, what equipment is running, what could go wrong next. It’s automatic before I’ve even had a chance to think.\n\nAnd it’s not just at work. At home, if the smoke alarm chirps or makes any little sound, I tense up right away. I know, logically, a low battery chirp isn’t the same thing, but my body doesn’t seem to know that. I’m already scanning the room, listening for more, waiting for some other sign that there’s danger.\n\nIt feels like I can’t just be in a place anymore. I’m always watching and listening for the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for what happened with the valve failure?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nPart of me wants to say no, because I know I didn’t make the valve fail with my own hands or anything like that. I keep telling myself that. But then my head goes back over every little thing, like… should I have noticed something earlier, should I have pushed harder, should I have stopped the line sooner, should I have been standing in a different place, should I have told somebody something differently.\n\nSo, responsible? I don’t know. I don’t know if that’s the right word. But I do keep going back through it like there’s some version where if I’d done one small thing differently, maybe it wouldn’t have gone the way it did. And I know that might not be rational, but it’s there. It’s hard not to feel like, as the person in charge of operations, I should’ve somehow prevented it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly aware of your surroundings, even when there's no apparent danger, in quiet or safe environments like your home?\n\nPatient: Yes. Especially at home, which is what bothers me, because home is supposed to be the place where I can shut all that off.\n\nIf the smoke alarm chirps, even just that little low-battery kind of chirp, my whole body tightens up before I even think. I’ll stop what I’m doing and start listening for more sounds, looking around the room, checking where the exits are, thinking, okay, what’s running, what could be overheating, what could fail. And I know it sounds excessive, because nothing is usually happening. It’s quiet, it’s safe, there’s no smoke, no emergency. But my body reacts like something is about to go wrong.\n\nIt’s the same kind of thing as when I hear a forklift beep or a compressor kick on at work — that sound hits me and I’m already scanning, already bracing. I don’t feel like I’m choosing to do it. It’s just automatic now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, or like things aren't real?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’d describe it exactly like being outside my body, like watching myself from across the room or anything.\n\nThere are moments where I feel kind of… not fully there, maybe? But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I’m so caught up in reacting or trying to figure out what’s happening that afterward it feels a little blurry, like I was on autopilot. But I can’t say I’ve had this clear feeling of floating above myself or looking down at myself.\n\nAnd as far as things not being real… I don’t know. I don’t think so. I mean, I know where I am. I know what’s going on. It’s more like I get overwhelmed or locked into what I’m noticing, not like the room turns unreal or anything. Maybe I’m misunderstanding the question, but I haven’t really noticed it in that specific way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when you're reliving the memory of the valve failure and the smoke in the aisle?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that I feel detached, exactly. When the memory comes back, it’s not like I’m looking at myself from the outside or like the room disappears or becomes unreal. I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way.\n\nIt’s more that I get pulled into the details of it, if that makes sense. Like my mind goes straight to the aisle, the smoke, the valve, what I was trying to figure out, what I should’ve done next. But I still know I’m here. I know where I am. I don’t feel separated from myself so much as… overwhelmed by it, or stuck on it for a bit.\n\nI guess I hesitate because maybe there are moments where I go quiet or feel kind of far away, but I don’t know if that’s detachment or just me being caught in the memory. I wouldn’t want to say yes if you mean something more specific than that.\n\nDoctor: When you're remembering what happened with the valve failure and the smoke in the aisle, do you ever feel like you're not really there or like it's not happening to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that exact way. When I remember it, it feels like it’s definitely happening to me, if that makes sense. I don’t feel like I’m watching some other person, or like I’m not really part of it.\n\nIt’s more like my head gets crowded with the scene — the aisle, the smoke, trying to understand what failed, what I was supposed to do — but I still know it’s my memory. I know I’m sitting here or standing in my kitchen or wherever I am at the time. I don’t lose track of that completely.\n\nMaybe I get quiet, or I zone in too hard on it, and afterward I might think, okay, I was kind of gone for a minute. But I don’t know that I’d call it “not really there.” I wouldn’t want to say yes to that and make it sound like I’m having some out-of-body thing, because I haven’t noticed that. It’s more like I’m too much there with the memory, not separate from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been deliberately avoiding any specific activities or places that remind you of the valve failure incident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve made a clear decision like, “I’m never going there again” or “I’m not doing that activity,” if that’s what you mean by deliberately avoiding.\n\nThere are definitely things that make me think of it, but I don’t know that I can point to a specific place I’ve been avoiding on purpose. Sometimes I hesitate or kind of drag my feet if something feels like it’s going to put me back in that mindset, but I don’t know if that counts. It’s not like I have a formal list in my head.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t noticed a specific activity or place that I’m consciously steering around because of the valve failure. If I am doing it, it may be more automatic than deliberate, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience flashbacks to the valve failure incident?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, that’s probably the right word for it. It doesn’t feel like just thinking about it on purpose. It can happen when I’m doing something completely normal, like sitting at the kitchen table, or trying to answer emails from home, and then suddenly it’s like I’m right back there.\n\nI’ll see that flash from the valve, and the smoke in the aisle, and it just kind of shoves everything else out of my head. Whatever I was doing a second before almost doesn’t matter for a moment. It’s not me choosing to replay it. It just comes in hard, and it’s very clear, like my mind snaps back to that scene before I can stop it.\n\nSometimes I’ll realize I’ve stopped typing or I’m just sitting there staring because I’m caught in that image again. It’s not like a normal memory where you can keep it at a distance. It feels immediate, like it takes over my attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the valve failure incident?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. “Guilty” is a strong word, and I’m not sure if what I feel is guilt or just… going over it again and again and wondering if I should have done something differently.\n\nI mean, I keep thinking about the sequence of it, like where I was, what I noticed first, what I understood in that moment versus what I understand now. And I’ll catch myself thinking, “Did I miss something? Was there some sign earlier? Should I have reacted faster?” But I don’t know if that means I feel guilty exactly, or if I’m just trying to make sense of it.\n\nI haven’t sat there and said, “This was my fault,” not in a straightforward way. But it’s hard not to have those thoughts creep in, especially because I’m used to being responsible for operations and keeping things under control. So when something like that happens, even if I don’t know that I caused it, my mind still goes to what I could’ve controlled.\n\nSo… maybe some guilt-adjacent feelings, if that makes sense. But I’m not sure I’d say plain guilt. It’s more questioning myself than a clear feeling that I did something wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential threats or dangers, even in situations where most people would feel safe?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yeah, I do. More than I used to, definitely.\n\nIt’s like my body reacts before I’ve even had time to think. Any time I hear a forklift backing up, that beeping sound, I tense up immediately. Or if a compressor kicks on and makes that sudden mechanical noise, I’m already scanning around, like, where is it coming from, is something failing, what’s the nearest way out, who’s standing too close to what. It’s not just at work either. Even at home, if the smoke alarm chirps because the battery is low, I feel my chest and shoulders tighten, and I’m looking around like something’s about to happen.\n\nI know, logically, that a smoke alarm chirp at home isn’t the same thing. Or a normal forklift beep doesn’t mean there’s danger right then. But in the moment it doesn’t feel normal. It feels like I have to catch the next thing before it catches me. I’ll start looking for exits or thinking through what could go wrong next, almost automatically. It’s exhausting, honestly, because most people probably just hear a noise and move on, and I’m already bracing like something bad is starting again.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If I am doing it, it may be more automatic than deliberate, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience flashbacks to the valve failure incident?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, that’s probably the right word for it. It doesn’t feel like just thinking about it on purpose. It can happen when I’m doing something completely normal, like sitting at the kitchen table, or trying to answer emails from home, and then suddenly it’s like I’m right back there.\n\nI’ll see that flash from the valve, and the smoke in the aisle, and it just kind of shoves everything else out of my head. Whatever I was doing a second before almost doesn’t matter for a moment. It’s not me choosing to replay it. It just comes in hard, and it’s very clear, like my mind snaps back to that scene before I can stop it.\n\nSometimes I’ll realize I’ve stopped typing or I’m just sitting there staring because I’m caught in that image again. It’s not like a normal memory where you can keep it at a distance. It feels immediate, like it takes over my attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the valve failure incident?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. “Guilty” is a strong word, and I’m not sure if what I feel is guilt or just… going over it again and again and wondering if I should have done something differently.\n\nI mean, I keep thinking about the sequence of it, like where I was, what I noticed first, what I understood in that moment versus what I understand now. And I’ll catch myself thinking, “Did I miss something? Was there some sign earlier? Should I have reacted faster?” But I don’t know if that means I feel guilty exactly, or if I’m just trying to make sense of it.\n\nI haven’t sat there and said, “This was my fault,” not in a straightforward way. But it’s hard not to have those thoughts creep in, especially because I’m used to being responsible for operations and keeping things under control. So when something like that happens, even if I don’t know that I caused it, my mind still goes to what I could’ve controlled.\n\nSo… maybe some guilt-adjacent feelings, if that makes sense. But I’m not sure I’d say plain guilt. It’s more questioning myself than a clear feeling that I did something wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential threats or dangers, even in situations where most people would feel safe?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yeah, I do. More than I used to, definitely.\n\nIt’s like my body reacts before I’ve even had time to think. Any time I hear a forklift backing up, that beeping sound, I tense up immediately. Or if a compressor kicks on and makes that sudden mechanical noise, I’m already scanning around, like, where is it coming from, is something failing, what’s the nearest way out, who’s standing too close to what. It’s not just at work either. Even at home, if the smoke alarm chirps because the battery is low, I feel my chest and shoulders tighten, and I’m looking around like something’s about to happen.\n\nI know, logically, that a smoke alarm chirp at home isn’t the same thing. Or a normal forklift beep doesn’t mean there’s danger right then. But in the moment it doesn’t feel normal. It feels like I have to catch the next thing before it catches me. I’ll start looking for exits or thinking through what could go wrong next, almost automatically. It’s exhausting, honestly, because most people probably just hear a noise and move on, and I’m already bracing like something bad is starting again."
